No, I wouldn't say I forget things that just happened. My memory is actually pretty sharp for the most part. Like, I can remember exactly what I had for breakfast this morning—two eggs, over easy, with a slice of sourdough toast. I get the sourdough from this bakery a couple of towns over. Right, since my cousin moved into our spare room after the surgery about 11 months ago, my wife's been trying to coordinate all his stuff, and keeping up with what she's saying has just gotten a lot worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself forgetting things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I forget things that just happened. My memory is actually pretty sharp for the most part. Like, I can remember exactly what I had for breakfast this morning—two eggs, over easy, with a slice of sourdough toast. I get the sourdough from this bakery a couple of towns over. The woman who owns it, she's actually from San Francisco originally, so she really knows her sourdough. She brought the starter with her when she moved here in the 90s. Can you imagine keeping a jar of dough alive for that long? It's like having a pet you have to feed flour to. I tried making my own once during the pandemic, you know, when everyone was doing it, but it just smelled like old gym socks and never bubbled right. But no, my short-term memory is fine. It's just... paying attention to the conversation in the first place that's the trick.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find your mind wandering to other things when you're in the middle of a conversation?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Well, no, not really. I don't feel like my mind wanders off to other things. It's more like... I'm just not catching it. But my wife, she's a master at multitasking. My memory is actually pretty sharp for the most part. Like, I can remember exactly what I had for breakfast this morning—two eggs, over easy, with a slice of sourdough toast. I get the sourdough from this bakery a couple of towns over. The woman who owns it, she's actually from San Francisco originally, so she really knows her sourdough. She brought the starter with her when she moved here in the 90s. Can you imagine keeping a jar of dough alive for that long? It's like having a pet you have to feed flour to. I tried making my own once during the pandemic, you know, when everyone was doing it, but it just smelled like old gym socks and never bubbled right. But no, my short-term memory is fine. It's just... paying attention to the conversation in the first place that's the trick.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind wandering to other things when you're in the middle of a conversation?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. She's obsessed with this one about cold cases in the Pacific Northwest. It's wild, they use DNA from like, distant cousins on ancestry websites to solve crimes from forty years ago. Anxious? No, I wouldn't say anxious. I mean, my wife gets anxious enough for the both of us, honestly. She watches the evening news every single night and just winds herself up about the economy and the weather and everything else. Did you see that storm system they were tracking last week? They made it sound like it was going to wash away the whole county, and all we got was a bit of drizzle on Thursday morning. I had already moved all the patio furniture into the garage, too. The heavy wrought-iron stuff. My back was aching for three days after that. I tried using that muscle rub cream, the one that smells like menthol? It works alright, but it gets all over the sheets, and then the whole bedroom smells like a locker room. 

But anyway, no, I don't get anxious about the conversation stuff. I just kind of shrug it off.

Right, since my cousin moved into our spare room after the surgery about 11 months ago, my wife's been trying to coordinate all his stuff, and keeping up with what she's saying has just gotten a lot worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself forgetting things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I forget things that just happened. My memory is actually pretty sharp for the most part. Like, I can remember exactly what I had for breakfast this morning—two eggs, over easy, with a slice of sourdough toast. I get the sourdough from this bakery a couple of towns over. The woman who owns it, she's actually from San Francisco originally, so she really knows her sourdough. She brought the starter with her when she moved here in the 90s. Can you imagine keeping a jar of dough alive for that long? It's like having a pet you have to feed flour to. I tried making my own once during the pandemic, you know, when everyone was doing it, but it just smelled like old gym socks and never bubbled right. But no, my short-term memory is fine. It's just... paying attention to the conversation in the first place that's the trick.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind wandering to other things when you're in the middle of a conversation?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't feel like my mind wanders off to other things. It's more like... I'm just not catching it. But my wife, she's a master at multitasking. She can be talking to me, chopping vegetables, and listening to her true crime podcasts all at the same time. Have you ever listened to those? She's obsessed with this one about cold cases in the Pacific Northwest. It's wild, they use DNA from like, distant cousins on ancestry websites to solve crimes from forty years ago. Makes you think twice about spitting in one of those tubes, doesn't it? I mean, who knows what your second cousin twice removed is up to. But yeah, no, my mind doesn't really wander.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention when you're doing things by yourself, like reading or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually. Like, if I sit down to work out a catering order or, you know, listen through a parish planning call... I usually start off just fine. I help out with the church down the road, and they always want me looking over the food logistics for the summer festival. We did a big fish fry last year, and let me tell you, finding decent cod in bulk right now? Good luck. The supplier we used for years just packed up and retired to Florida. Naples, I think? Or maybe Fort Myers. Somewhere on the Gulf side. The water down there is so warm, it's like bathwater. Not like the ocean up here where you freeze your toes off just wading in. My wife and I went to Sanibel Island down there once, years ago, and found the most incredible seashells... \n\nBut, uh, anyway. The calls and the catering. Right. I'll be doing that, and before long my mind just completely wanders off, and I end up having to go back over the exact same notes, like I never even read them in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or stressed when you're unable to follow conversations?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say overwhelmed, exactly. It's more... well, maybe a little frustrating? But it's not like I'm pulling my hair out over it. I mean, stress is a funny thing, isn't it? Like, my brother-in-law, he gets stressed about everything. He's an accountant, and tax season just wrecks him. He gets this little twitch under his left eye right around mid-March, and it doesn't stop until May. He actually tried acupuncture for it last year. Can you believe that? Letting someone stick all those tiny needles in your face. He said it didn't hurt, but I don't know, I'm not a fan of needles. I remember when I had to get my blood drawn for a cholesterol check a few months back, the nurse couldn't find the vein. She had to poke me three times. My arm looked like a pincushion for a week. \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't really get stressed about the conversation thing. I just kind of... roll with it, I guess. It is what it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you have trouble following conversations?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, I wouldn't say anxious. I mean, my wife gets anxious enough for the both of us, honestly. She watches the evening news every single night and just winds herself up about the economy and the weather and everything else. Did you see that storm system they were tracking last week? They made it sound like it was going to wash away the whole county, and all we got was a bit of drizzle on Thursday morning. I had already moved all the patio furniture into the garage, too. The heavy wrought-iron stuff. My back was aching for three days after that. I tried using that muscle rub cream, the one that smells like menthol? It works alright, but it gets all over the sheets, and then the whole bedroom smells like a locker room. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't get anxious about the conversation stuff. I just kind of shrug it off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Not caring? Oh, no, it's not that I don't care. I love my wife, and I want to hear what she has to say. It's just... I don't know, sometimes my brain just kind of takes a detour. That's when he actually moved in with us, my cousin, I mean, after his surgery. He had to get his knee done. The recovery on those things, I tell you, it's no joke. He was in the rehab center for a bit first, and the food there was just abysmal. I'd bring him sandwiches from that little deli down on 4th street—the one with the green awning? They make a pastrami that is just out of this world. Piled high, with the really spicy mustard. The guy who runs it, Sal, he's been there for thirty years, always remembers your order... But, uh, yeah, what was I saying? Right, since my cousin moved into our spare room after the surgery about 11 months ago, my wife's been trying to coordinate all his stuff, and keeping up with what she's saying has just gotten a lot worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself forgetting things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I forget things that just happened. My memory is actually pretty sharp for the most part. Like, I can remember exactly what I had for breakfast this morning—two eggs, over easy, with a slice of sourdough toast. I get the sourdough from this bakery a couple of towns over. The woman who owns it, she's actually from San Francisco originally, so she really knows her sourdough. She brought the starter with her when she moved here in the 90s. Can you imagine keeping a jar of dough alive for that long? It's like having a pet you have to feed flour to. I tried making my own once during the pandemic, you know, when everyone was doing it, but it just smelled like old gym socks and never bubbled right. But no, my short-term memory is fine. It's just... paying attention to the conversation in the first place that's the trick.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind wandering to other things when you're in the middle of a conversation?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't feel like my mind wanders off to other things. It's more like... I'm just not catching it. But my wife, she's a master at multitasking. She can be talking to me, chopping vegetables, and listening to her true crime podcasts all at the same time. Have you ever listened to those? She's obsessed with this one about cold cases in the Pacific Northwest. It's wild, they use DNA from like, distant cousins on ancestry websites to solve crimes from forty years ago. Makes you think twice about spitting in one of those tubes, doesn't it? I mean, who knows what your second cousin twice removed is up to. But yeah, no, my mind doesn't really wander.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention when you're doing things by yourself, like reading or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually. Like, if I sit down to work out a catering order or, you know, listen through a parish planning call... I usually start off just fine. I help out with the church down the road, and they always want me looking over the food logistics for the summer festival. We did a big fish fry last year, and let me tell you, finding decent cod in bulk right now? Good luck. The supplier we used for years just packed up and retired to Florida. Naples, I think? Or maybe Fort Myers. Somewhere on the Gulf side. The water down there is so warm, it's like bathwater. Not like the ocean up here where you freeze your toes off just wading in. My wife and I went to Sanibel Island down there once, years ago, and found the most incredible seashells... \n\nBut, uh, anyway. The calls and the catering. Right. I'll be doing that, and before long my mind just completely wanders off, and I end up having to go back over the exact same notes, like I never even read them in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or stressed when you're unable to follow conversations?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say overwhelmed, exactly. It's more... well, maybe a little frustrating? But it's not like I'm pulling my hair out over it. I mean, stress is a funny thing, isn't it? Like, my brother-in-law, he gets stressed about everything. He's an accountant, and tax season just wrecks him. He gets this little twitch under his left eye right around mid-March, and it doesn't stop until May. He actually tried acupuncture for it last year. Can you believe that? Letting someone stick all those tiny needles in your face. He said it didn't hurt, but I don't know, I'm not a fan of needles. I remember when I had to get my blood drawn for a cholesterol check a few months back, the nurse couldn't find the vein. She had to poke me three times. My arm looked like a pincushion for a week. \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't really get stressed about the conversation thing. I just kind of... roll with it, I guess. It is what it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you have trouble following conversations?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, I wouldn't say anxious. I mean, my wife gets anxious enough for the both of us, honestly. She watches the evening news every single night and just winds herself up about the economy and the weather and everything else. Did you see that storm system they were tracking last week? They made it sound like it was going to wash away the whole county, and all we got was a bit of drizzle on Thursday morning. I had already moved all the patio furniture into the garage, too. The heavy wrought-iron stuff. My back was aching for three days after that. I tried using that muscle rub cream, the one that smells like menthol? It works alright, but it gets all over the sheets, and then the whole bedroom smells like a locker room. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't get anxious about the conversation stuff. I just kind of shrug it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself not caring about the topics your wife is discussing when you have trouble following her?\n\nPatient: Not caring? Oh, no, it's not that I don't care. I love my wife, and I want to hear what she has to say. It's just... I don't know, sometimes my brain just kind of takes a detour. Like yesterday, she was telling me about her sister's new golden retriever puppy. They named him Buster. Which is a cute name, I guess, but it always makes me think of that old Buster Keaton movie. Have you ever seen *The General*? It's a silent film, but the stunts he did on that moving train are just incredible. They didn't have CGI back then, you know. If a guy fell off a train, he actually fell off a train. I read a book about early Hollywood a few years ago, and the safety regulations were practically non-existent. They used real ammunition in some of those Westerns! Can you imagine? \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my wife. Yeah, no, I care about what she's saying. I just... I don't know, I just drift away sometimes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Well, I suppose it does cause a few hiccups here and there. Like, it's definitely caused some tension at home lately. My wife, she feels like she can't really count on me when it comes to helping out with my cousin's care. My cousin, he's been having a rough go of it since his surgery. I tried sitting still for a whole afternoon once when I pulled a hamstring playing softball—this was years ago, back when I still played in that church league—and I ended up organizing the entire garage because I just couldn't sit on the couch anymore. Found a box of old vinyl records I thought I'd lost in the move. Had my original pressing of Abbey Road in there. 

But anyway, yeah, she gets upset because I miss things she tells me about his schedule. Like last week, I almost brought vegetarian quiches to a barbecue joint's anniversary party. Could have been a disaster.

I tried making my own once during the pandemic, you know, when everyone was doing it, but it just smelled like old gym socks and never bubbled right. But no, my short-term memory is fine. It's just... paying attention to the conversation in the first place that's the trick.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind wandering to other things when you're in the middle of a conversation?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't feel like my mind wanders off to other things. It's more like... I'm just not catching it. But my wife, she's a master at multitasking. She can be talking to me, chopping vegetables, and listening to her true crime podcasts all at the same time. Have you ever listened to those? She's obsessed with this one about cold cases in the Pacific Northwest. It's wild, they use DNA from like, distant cousins on ancestry websites to solve crimes from forty years ago. Makes you think twice about spitting in one of those tubes, doesn't it? I mean, who knows what your second cousin twice removed is up to. But yeah, no, my mind doesn't really wander.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention when you're doing things by yourself, like reading or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually. Like, if I sit down to work out a catering order or, you know, listen through a parish planning call... I usually start off just fine. I help out with the church down the road, and they always want me looking over the food logistics for the summer festival. We did a big fish fry last year, and let me tell you, finding decent cod in bulk right now? Good luck. The supplier we used for years just packed up and retired to Florida. Naples, I think? Or maybe Fort Myers. Somewhere on the Gulf side. The water down there is so warm, it's like bathwater. Not like the ocean up here where you freeze your toes off just wading in. My wife and I went to Sanibel Island down there once, years ago, and found the most incredible seashells... \n\nBut, uh, anyway. The calls and the catering. Right. I'll be doing that, and before long my mind just completely wanders off, and I end up having to go back over the exact same notes, like I never even read them in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or stressed when you're unable to follow conversations?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say overwhelmed, exactly. It's more... well, maybe a little frustrating? But it's not like I'm pulling my hair out over it. I mean, stress is a funny thing, isn't it? Like, my brother-in-law, he gets stressed about everything. He's an accountant, and tax season just wrecks him. He gets this little twitch under his left eye right around mid-March, and it doesn't stop until May. He actually tried acupuncture for it last year. Can you believe that? Letting someone stick all those tiny needles in your face. He said it didn't hurt, but I don't know, I'm not a fan of needles. I remember when I had to get my blood drawn for a cholesterol check a few months back, the nurse couldn't find the vein. She had to poke me three times. My arm looked like a pincushion for a week. \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't really get stressed about the conversation thing. I just kind of... roll with it, I guess. It is what it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you have trouble following conversations?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, I wouldn't say anxious. I mean, my wife gets anxious enough for the both of us, honestly. She watches the evening news every single night and just winds herself up about the economy and the weather and everything else. Did you see that storm system they were tracking last week? They made it sound like it was going to wash away the whole county, and all we got was a bit of drizzle on Thursday morning. I had already moved all the patio furniture into the garage, too. The heavy wrought-iron stuff. My back was aching for three days after that. I tried using that muscle rub cream, the one that smells like menthol? Oh, no, it's not that I don't care. I love my wife, and I want to hear what she has to say. It's just... I don't know, sometimes my brain just kind of takes a detour. Like yesterday, she was telling me about her sister's new golden retriever puppy. They named him Buster. Which is a cute name, I guess, but it always makes me think of that old Buster Keaton movie. Have you ever seen *The General*? It's a silent film, but the stunts he did on that moving train are just incredible. They didn't have CGI back then, you know. If a guy fell off a train, he actually fell off a train. I read a book about early Hollywood a few years ago, and the safety regulations were practically non-existent. They used real ammunition in some of those Westerns! Can you imagine? \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my wife. Yeah, no, I care about what she's saying. I just... I don't know, I just drift away sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or meeting responsibilities due to this issue with following conversations?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does cause a few hiccups here and there. Like, it's definitely caused some tension at home lately. My wife, she feels like she can't really count on me when it comes to helping out with my cousin's care. My cousin, he's been having a rough go of it since his surgery. They put pins in his ankle, and he's not supposed to put any weight on it for six weeks. Six weeks! Can you imagine just sitting around that long? I'd go stir crazy. I tried sitting still for a whole afternoon once when I pulled a hamstring playing softball—this was years ago, back when I still played in that church league—and I ended up organizing the entire garage because I just couldn't sit on the couch anymore. Found a box of old vinyl records I thought I'd lost in the move. Had my original pressing of Abbey Road in there. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, she gets upset because I miss things she tells me about his schedule. And with my catering jobs—just small gigs, you know, I pick them up here and there—I've had to start double-checking everything or even redoing things because I'll miss a detail in the conversation with the client and get the small jobs mixed up. Like last week, I almost brought vegetarian quiches to a barbecue joint's anniversary party. Could have been a disaster.\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty following conversations affected your relationship with your wife or other family members?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, yeah, it really has. The tension at home is definitely there right now. She just feels like she can’t count on me when it comes to my cousin’s care, which makes her feel like she has to do it all herself. And I feel bad about it, I really do. My cousin is a good guy. He actually helped me build the deck on the back of our house a few summers ago. We used that composite decking material? It’s supposed to last forever and you never have to stain it. Though, honestly, it gets incredibly hot in the afternoon sun. You can't even walk out there barefoot in July or you'll burn your soles right off. We ended up having to buy this massive outdoor rug just so the dog could go out the back door without doing a little tap dance. \n\nBut, uh, yeah... it definitely affects our relationship. And with my catering jobs—just small gigs, you know, I pick them up here and there—I've had to start double-checking everything or even redoing things because I'll miss a detail in the conversation with the client and get the small jobs mixed up. Like last week, I almost brought vegetarian quiches to a barbecue joint's anniversary party. Could have been a disaster.\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty following conversations affected your relationship with your wife or other family members?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, yeah, it really has. The tension at home is definitely there right now. She just feels like she can’t count on me when it comes to my cousin’s care, which makes her feel like she has to do it all herself. And I feel bad about it, I really do. My cousin is a good guy. He actually helped me build the deck on the back of our house a few summers ago. We used that composite decking material? It’s supposed to last forever and you never have to stain it. Though, honestly, it gets incredibly hot in the afternoon sun. You can't even walk out there barefoot in July or you'll burn your soles right off. We ended up having to buy this massive outdoor rug just so the dog could go out the back door without doing a little tap dance. \n\nBut, uh, yeah... it definitely affects our relationship. Her being frustrated about the cousin situation, and then seeing me have to constantly double-check everything or redo my prep work for those catering tasks so I don't mix up the small jobs... it just adds to the stress between us, you know?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would have trouble following conversations if they were about topics that really interest you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, that's a good question. I mean, if it's something I'm really into, like... say, vintage car restoration. I love old cars. I was actually just reading this article about a guy who found a 1969 Mustang Mach 1 sitting in a barn for forty years. Forty years! Can you believe that? The original paint was completely rusted over, but the engine block was still solid. Those old Ford engines, they really built them to last. My dad had a Ford pickup when I was a kid, an F-150, I think. We used to take it out to the lake on weekends and fish for bluegill. I haven't been fishing in ages, though. You need a lot of patience for fishing, just sitting there waiting for a bite. 

But, uh, what were you asking? Oh, right, following conversations about things I like. You know, I'm honestly not sure. I guess I haven't really paid attention to whether it happens with stuff I'm interested in. I just know it happens a lot with the day-to-day stuff.

She's obsessed with this one about cold cases in the Pacific Northwest. It's wild, they use DNA from like, distant cousins on ancestry websites to solve crimes from forty years ago. Like, if I sit down to work out a catering order or, you know, listen through a parish planning call... I usually start off just fine. I help out with the church down the road, and they always want me looking over the food logistics for the summer festival. Or maybe Fort Myers. Somewhere on the Gulf side. The water down there is so warm, it's like bathwater. Not like the ocean up here where you freeze your toes off just wading in. He gets this little twitch under his left eye right around mid-March, and it doesn't stop until May. He actually tried acupuncture for it last year. Can you believe that? My arm looked like a pincushion for a week. \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't really get stressed about the conversation thing. I just kind of... roll with it, I guess. It is what it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you have trouble following conversations?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, I wouldn't say anxious. Right, since my cousin moved into our spare room after the surgery about 11 months ago, my wife's been trying to coordinate all his stuff, and keeping up with what she's saying has just gotten a lot worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself forgetting things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I forget things that just happened. My memory is actually pretty sharp for the most part. Like, I can remember exactly what I had for breakfast this morning—two eggs, over easy, with a slice of sourdough toast. I get the sourdough from this bakery a couple of towns over. The woman who owns it, she's actually from San Francisco originally, so she really knows her sourdough. She brought the starter with her when she moved here in the 90s. Can you imagine keeping a jar of dough alive for that long? It's like having a pet you have to feed flour to. I tried making my own once during the pandemic, you know, when everyone was doing it, but it just smelled like old gym socks and never bubbled right. But no, my short-term memory is fine. It's just... paying attention to the conversation in the first place that's the trick.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind wandering to other things when you're in the middle of a conversation?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't feel like my mind wanders off to other things. It's more like... I'm just not catching it. But my wife, she's a master at multitasking. She can be talking to me, chopping vegetables, and listening to her true crime podcasts all at the same time. Have you ever listened to those? She's obsessed with this one about cold cases in the Pacific Northwest. It's wild, they use DNA from like, distant cousins on ancestry websites to solve crimes from forty years ago. Makes you think twice about spitting in one of those tubes, doesn't it? I mean, who knows what your second cousin twice removed is up to. But yeah, no, my mind doesn't really wander.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention when you're doing things by yourself, like reading or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually. Like, if I sit down to work out a catering order or, you know, listen through a parish planning call... I usually start off just fine. I help out with the church down the road, and they always want me looking over the food logistics for the summer festival. We did a big fish fry last year, and let me tell you, finding decent cod in bulk right now? Good luck. The supplier we used for years just packed up and retired to Florida. Naples, I think? Or maybe Fort Myers. Somewhere on the Gulf side. The water down there is so warm, it's like bathwater. Not like the ocean up here where you freeze your toes off just wading in. My wife and I went to Sanibel Island down there once, years ago, and found the most incredible seashells... \n\nBut, uh, anyway. The calls and the catering. Right. I'll be doing that, and before long my mind just completely wanders off, and I end up having to go back over the exact same notes, like I never even read them in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or stressed when you're unable to follow conversations?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say overwhelmed, exactly. It's more... well, maybe a little frustrating? But it's not like I'm pulling my hair out over it. I mean, stress is a funny thing, isn't it? Like, my brother-in-law, he gets stressed about everything. He's an accountant, and tax season just wrecks him. He gets this little twitch under his left eye right around mid-March, and it doesn't stop until May. He actually tried acupuncture for it last year. Can you believe that? Letting someone stick all those tiny needles in your face. He said it didn't hurt, but I don't know, I'm not a fan of needles. I remember when I had to get my blood drawn for a cholesterol check a few months back, the nurse couldn't find the vein. She had to poke me three times. My arm looked like a pincushion for a week. \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't really get stressed about the conversation thing. I just kind of... roll with it, I guess. It is what it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you have trouble following conversations?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, I wouldn't say anxious. I mean, my wife gets anxious enough for the both of us, honestly. She watches the evening news every single night and just winds herself up about the economy and the weather and everything else. Did you see that storm system they were tracking last week? They made it sound like it was going to wash away the whole county, and all we got was a bit of drizzle on Thursday morning. I had already moved all the patio furniture into the garage, too. The heavy wrought-iron stuff. My back was aching for three days after that. I tried using that muscle rub cream, the one that smells like menthol? It works alright, but it gets all over the sheets, and then the whole bedroom smells like a locker room. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't get anxious about the conversation stuff. I just kind of shrug it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself not caring about the topics your wife is discussing when you have trouble following her?\n\nPatient: Not caring? Oh, no, it's not that I don't care. I love my wife, and I want to hear what she has to say. It's just... I don't know, sometimes my brain just kind of takes a detour. Like yesterday, she was telling me about her sister's new golden retriever puppy. They named him Buster. Which is a cute name, I guess, but it always makes me think of that old Buster Keaton movie. Have you ever seen *The General*? It's a silent film, but the stunts he did on that moving train are just incredible. They didn't have CGI back then, you know. If a guy fell off a train, he actually fell off a train. I read a book about early Hollywood a few years ago, and the safety regulations were practically non-existent. They used real ammunition in some of those Westerns! Can you imagine? \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my wife. Yeah, no, I care about what she's saying. I just... I don't know, I just drift away sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or meeting responsibilities due to this issue with following conversations?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does cause a few hiccups here and there. Like, it's definitely caused some tension at home lately. My wife, she feels like she can't really count on me when it comes to helping out with my cousin's care. My cousin, he's been having a rough go of it since his surgery. They put pins in his ankle, and he's not supposed to put any weight on it for six weeks. Six weeks! Can you imagine just sitting around that long? I'd go stir crazy. I tried sitting still for a whole afternoon once when I pulled a hamstring playing softball—this was years ago, back when I still played in that church league—and I ended up organizing the entire garage because I just couldn't sit on the couch anymore. Found a box of old vinyl records I thought I'd lost in the move. Had my original pressing of Abbey Road in there. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, she gets upset because I miss things she tells me about his schedule. And with my catering jobs—just small gigs, you know, I pick them up here and there—I've had to start double-checking everything or even redoing things because I'll miss a detail in the conversation with the client and get the small jobs mixed up. Like last week, I almost brought vegetarian quiches to a barbecue joint's anniversary party. Could have been a disaster.\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty following conversations affected your relationship with your wife or other family members?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, yeah, it really has. The tension at home is definitely there right now. She just feels like she can’t count on me when it comes to my cousin’s care, which makes her feel like she has to do it all herself. And I feel bad about it, I really do. My cousin is a good guy. He actually helped me build the deck on the back of our house a few summers ago. We used that composite decking material? It’s supposed to last forever and you never have to stain it. Though, honestly, it gets incredibly hot in the afternoon sun. You can't even walk out there barefoot in July or you'll burn your soles right off. We ended up having to buy this massive outdoor rug just so the dog could go out the back door without doing a little tap dance. \n\nBut, uh, yeah... it definitely affects our relationship. Her being frustrated about the cousin situation, and then seeing me have to constantly double-check everything or redo my prep work for those catering tasks so I don't mix up the small jobs... it just adds to the stress between us, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have trouble following conversations if they were about topics that really interest you?\n\nPatient: Well, that's a good question. I mean, if it's something I'm really into, like... say, vintage car restoration. I love old cars. I was actually just reading this article about a guy who found a 1969 Mustang Mach 1 sitting in a barn for forty years. Forty years! Can you believe that? The original paint was completely rusted over, but the engine block was still solid. Those old Ford engines, they really built them to last. My dad had a Ford pickup when I was a kid, an F-150, I think. We used to take it out to the lake on weekends and fish for bluegill. I haven't been fishing in ages, though. You need a lot of patience for fishing, just sitting there waiting for a bite. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, right, following conversations about things I like. You know, I'm honestly not sure. I guess I haven't really paid attention to whether it happens with stuff I'm interested in. I'm really not sure. I haven't paid that much attention to it. I mean, if someone's talking to me about, say, the history of those old Ford engines, I might be able to track it better. But then again, maybe not. I was talking to a buddy at the hardware store the other day—he was telling me about this new type of drill bit that's supposed to cut through masonry like butter. Masonry bits are usually so brittle, you know? You put a little too much pressure on them and they just snap right in half. I went through three of them trying to hang a trellis on the brick wall out by the patio last spring. My wife wanted to grow climbing roses. Well, honestly, doc, you're asking me the same thing again, and I really just don't know. I haven't paid attention to whether it gets better if I'm interested. I mean, maybe? But it's hard to say. I help out with the church down the road, and they always want me looking over the food logistics for the summer festival. Or maybe Fort Myers. Somewhere on the Gulf side. The water down there is so warm, it's like bathwater. Not like the ocean up here where you freeze your toes off just wading in. My wife and I went to Sanibel Island down there once, years ago, and found the most incredible seashells... \n\nBut, uh, anyway. The calls and the catering. Right. I'll be doing that, and before long my mind just completely wanders off, and I end up having to go back over the exact same notes, like I never even read them in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or stressed when you're unable to follow conversations?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say overwhelmed, exactly. It's more... well, maybe a little frustrating? But it's not like I'm pulling my hair out over it. I mean, stress is a funny thing, isn't it? Like, my brother-in-law, he gets stressed about everything. He's an accountant, and tax season just wrecks him. He gets this little twitch under his left eye right around mid-March, and it doesn't stop until May. He actually tried acupuncture for it last year. Can you believe that? Letting someone stick all those tiny needles in your face. He said it didn't hurt, but I don't know, I'm not a fan of needles. I remember when I had to get my blood drawn for a cholesterol check a few months back, the nurse couldn't find the vein. She had to poke me three times. My arm looked like a pincushion for a week. \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't really get stressed about the conversation thing. I just kind of... roll with it, I guess. It is what it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you have trouble following conversations?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, I wouldn't say anxious. I mean, my wife gets anxious enough for the both of us, honestly. She watches the evening news every single night and just winds herself up about the economy and the weather and everything else. Did you see that storm system they were tracking last week? They made it sound like it was going to wash away the whole county, and all we got was a bit of drizzle on Thursday morning. I had already moved all the patio furniture into the garage, too. The heavy wrought-iron stuff. My back was aching for three days after that. I tried using that muscle rub cream, the one that smells like menthol? It works alright, but it gets all over the sheets, and then the whole bedroom smells like a locker room. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't get anxious about the conversation stuff. I just kind of shrug it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself not caring about the topics your wife is discussing when you have trouble following her?\n\nPatient: Not caring? Oh, no, it's not that I don't care. I love my wife, and I want to hear what she has to say. It's just... I don't know, sometimes my brain just kind of takes a detour. Like yesterday, she was telling me about her sister's new golden retriever puppy. They named him Buster. Which is a cute name, I guess, but it always makes me think of that old Buster Keaton movie. Have you ever seen *The General*? It's a silent film, but the stunts he did on that moving train are just incredible. They didn't have CGI back then, you know. If a guy fell off a train, he actually fell off a train. I read a book about early Hollywood a few years ago, and the safety regulations were practically non-existent. They used real ammunition in some of those Westerns! Can you imagine? \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my wife. Yeah, no, I care about what she's saying. I just... I don't know, I just drift away sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or meeting responsibilities due to this issue with following conversations?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does cause a few hiccups here and there. Like, it's definitely caused some tension at home lately. My wife, she feels like she can't really count on me when it comes to helping out with my cousin's care. My cousin, he's been having a rough go of it since his surgery. They put pins in his ankle, and he's not supposed to put any weight on it for six weeks. Six weeks! Can you imagine just sitting around that long? I'd go stir crazy. I tried sitting still for a whole afternoon once when I pulled a hamstring playing softball—this was years ago, back when I still played in that church league—and I ended up organizing the entire garage because I just couldn't sit on the couch anymore. Found a box of old vinyl records I thought I'd lost in the move. Had my original pressing of Abbey Road in there. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, she gets upset because I miss things she tells me about his schedule. And with my catering jobs—just small gigs, you know, I pick them up here and there—I've had to start double-checking everything or even redoing things because I'll miss a detail in the conversation with the client and get the small jobs mixed up. Like last week, I almost brought vegetarian quiches to a barbecue joint's anniversary party. Could have been a disaster.\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty following conversations affected your relationship with your wife or other family members?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, yeah, it really has. The tension at home is definitely there right now. She just feels like she can’t count on me when it comes to my cousin’s care, which makes her feel like she has to do it all herself. And I feel bad about it, I really do. My cousin is a good guy. He actually helped me build the deck on the back of our house a few summers ago. We used that composite decking material? It’s supposed to last forever and you never have to stain it. Though, honestly, it gets incredibly hot in the afternoon sun. You can't even walk out there barefoot in July or you'll burn your soles right off. We ended up having to buy this massive outdoor rug just so the dog could go out the back door without doing a little tap dance. \n\nBut, uh, yeah... it definitely affects our relationship. Her being frustrated about the cousin situation, and then seeing me have to constantly double-check everything or redo my prep work for those catering tasks so I don't mix up the small jobs... it just adds to the stress between us, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have trouble following conversations if they were about topics that really interest you?\n\nPatient: Well, that's a good question. I was actually just reading this article about a guy who found a 1969 Mustang Mach 1 sitting in a barn for forty years. Forty years! Can you believe that? The original paint was completely rusted over, but the engine block was still solid. Those old Ford engines, they really built them to last. My dad had a Ford pickup when I was a kid, an F-150, I think. We used to take it out to the lake on weekends and fish for bluegill. I haven't been fishing in ages, though. You need a lot of patience for fishing, just sitting there waiting for a bite. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, right, following conversations about things I like. You know, I'm honestly not sure. I guess I haven't really paid attention to whether it happens with stuff I'm interested in. I just know it happens a lot with the day-to-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to follow conversations about topics that you find very interesting?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying... But then again, maybe not. I was talking to a buddy at the hardware store the other day—he was telling me about this new type of drill bit that's supposed to cut through masonry like butter. Masonry bits are usually so brittle, you know? You put a little too much pressure on them and they just snap right in half. I went through three of them trying to hang a trellis on the brick wall out by the patio last spring. My wife wanted to grow climbing roses. I love those history shows. The sheer logistics of moving all those troops across the English Channel for D-Day is just mind-boggling. They had to coordinate the tides, the weather, the paratroopers dropping in behind enemy lines in the pitch black. And the Higgins boats! Those landing crafts were actually designed by a guy who made boats for the swamps down in Louisiana. They were originally meant for shallow water, pulling logs out of the bayou, and then the military realized they were perfect for running right up onto the beaches at Normandy. \n\nBut, um... yeah, to answer your question, I really couldn't tell you if I follow things better when it's interesting. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, I feel like we're going in circles here, because I really just don't have an answer for you. I don't know if being passionate about it makes a difference. I just haven't paid attention. It's like... well, take gardening. I'm pretty passionate about my tomatoes. I've got these heirloom varieties this year, Cherokee Purples and Brandywine. The soil pH has to be just right, you know? You can't just stick them in the dirt and hope for the best. I had to mix in a bunch of compost and some bone meal before the last frost. My neighbor, Gary, he uses that chemical fertilizer stuff, the blue powder you mix with water, and his plants always look huge, but the tomatoes themselves taste like water. There's no flavor. You gotta have good soil if you want the flavor.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, F88\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's my wife who pushed me to come. What really brought me in is that... well, she'll be telling me about something, like my cousin’s appointments or, you know, who's giving him rides. My cousin, he used to drive this old Chevy, beautiful car, cherry red, but he had to sell it a few years back when his eyesight started going. Such a shame, really, they don't make them like that anymore. Anyway, my wife will be talking to me about his schedule, and I’m looking right at her, nodding along, but a minute later I realize I didn’t actually take in half of what she said. It's like the words just wash right over me.\n\nDoctor: Has this trouble following conversations always been something you've experienced, or is it a relatively new problem for you?\n\nPatient: Well, I think... it's been noticeably harder for about 11 months now. That's when he actually moved in with us, my cousin, I mean, after his surgery. He had to get his knee done. The recovery on those things, I tell you, it's no joke. He was in the rehab center for a bit first, and the food there was just abysmal. I'd bring him sandwiches from that little deli down on 4th street—the one with the green awning? They make a pastrami that is just out of this world. Piled high, with the really spicy mustard. The guy who runs it, Sal, he's been there for thirty years, always remembers your order... But, uh, yeah, what was I saying? Right, since my cousin moved into our spare room after the surgery about 11 months ago, my wife's been trying to coordinate all his stuff, and keeping up with what she's saying has just gotten a lot worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself forgetting things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I forget things that just happened. My memory is actually pretty sharp for the most part. Like, I can remember exactly what I had for breakfast this morning—two eggs, over easy, with a slice of sourdough toast. I get the sourdough from this bakery a couple of towns over. The woman who owns it, she's actually from San Francisco originally, so she really knows her sourdough. She brought the starter with her when she moved here in the 90s. Can you imagine keeping a jar of dough alive for that long? It's like having a pet you have to feed flour to. I tried making my own once during the pandemic, you know, when everyone was doing it, but it just smelled like old gym socks and never bubbled right. But no, my short-term memory is fine. It's just... paying attention to the conversation in the first place that's the trick.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind wandering to other things when you're in the middle of a conversation?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't feel like my mind wanders off to other things. It's more like... I'm just not catching it. But my wife, she's a master at multitasking. She can be talking to me, chopping vegetables, and listening to her true crime podcasts all at the same time. Have you ever listened to those? She's obsessed with this one about cold cases in the Pacific Northwest. It's wild, they use DNA from like, distant cousins on ancestry websites to solve crimes from forty years ago. Makes you think twice about spitting in one of those tubes, doesn't it? I mean, who knows what your second cousin twice removed is up to. But yeah, no, my mind doesn't really wander.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention when you're doing things by yourself, like reading or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually. Like, if I sit down to work out a catering order or, you know, listen through a parish planning call... I usually start off just fine. I help out with the church down the road, and they always want me looking over the food logistics for the summer festival. We did a big fish fry last year, and let me tell you, finding decent cod in bulk right now? Good luck. The supplier we used for years just packed up and retired to Florida. Naples, I think? Or maybe Fort Myers. Somewhere on the Gulf side. The water down there is so warm, it's like bathwater. Not like the ocean up here where you freeze your toes off just wading in. My wife and I went to Sanibel Island down there once, years ago, and found the most incredible seashells... \n\nBut, uh, anyway. The calls and the catering. Right. I'll be doing that, and before long my mind just completely wanders off, and I end up having to go back over the exact same notes, like I never even read them in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or stressed when you're unable to follow conversations?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say overwhelmed, exactly. It's more... well, maybe a little frustrating? But it's not like I'm pulling my hair out over it. I mean, stress is a funny thing, isn't it? Like, my brother-in-law, he gets stressed about everything. He's an accountant, and tax season just wrecks him. He gets this little twitch under his left eye right around mid-March, and it doesn't stop until May. He actually tried acupuncture for it last year. Can you believe that? Letting someone stick all those tiny needles in your face. He said it didn't hurt, but I don't know, I'm not a fan of needles. I remember when I had to get my blood drawn for a cholesterol check a few months back, the nurse couldn't find the vein. She had to poke me three times. My arm looked like a pincushion for a week. \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't really get stressed about the conversation thing. I just kind of... roll with it, I guess. It is what it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you have trouble following conversations?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, I wouldn't say anxious. I mean, my wife gets anxious enough for the both of us, honestly. She watches the evening news every single night and just winds herself up about the economy and the weather and everything else. Did you see that storm system they were tracking last week? They made it sound like it was going to wash away the whole county, and all we got was a bit of drizzle on Thursday morning. I had already moved all the patio furniture into the garage, too. The heavy wrought-iron stuff. My back was aching for three days after that. I tried using that muscle rub cream, the one that smells like menthol? It works alright, but it gets all over the sheets, and then the whole bedroom smells like a locker room. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't get anxious about the conversation stuff. I just kind of shrug it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself not caring about the topics your wife is discussing when you have trouble following her?\n\nPatient: Not caring? Oh, no, it's not that I don't care. I love my wife, and I want to hear what she has to say. It's just... I don't know, sometimes my brain just kind of takes a detour. Like yesterday, she was telling me about her sister's new golden retriever puppy. They named him Buster. Which is a cute name, I guess, but it always makes me think of that old Buster Keaton movie. Have you ever seen *The General*? It's a silent film, but the stunts he did on that moving train are just incredible. They didn't have CGI back then, you know. If a guy fell off a train, he actually fell off a train. I read a book about early Hollywood a few years ago, and the safety regulations were practically non-existent. They used real ammunition in some of those Westerns! Can you imagine? \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my wife. Yeah, no, I care about what she's saying. I just... I don't know, I just drift away sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or meeting responsibilities due to this issue with following conversations?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does cause a few hiccups here and there. Like, it's definitely caused some tension at home lately. My wife, she feels like she can't really count on me when it comes to helping out with my cousin's care. My cousin, he's been having a rough go of it since his surgery. They put pins in his ankle, and he's not supposed to put any weight on it for six weeks. Six weeks! Can you imagine just sitting around that long? I'd go stir crazy. I tried sitting still for a whole afternoon once when I pulled a hamstring playing softball—this was years ago, back when I still played in that church league—and I ended up organizing the entire garage because I just couldn't sit on the couch anymore. Found a box of old vinyl records I thought I'd lost in the move. Had my original pressing of Abbey Road in there. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, she gets upset because I miss things she tells me about his schedule. And with my catering jobs—just small gigs, you know, I pick them up here and there—I've had to start double-checking everything or even redoing things because I'll miss a detail in the conversation with the client and get the small jobs mixed up. Like last week, I almost brought vegetarian quiches to a barbecue joint's anniversary party. Could have been a disaster.\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty following conversations affected your relationship with your wife or other family members?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, yeah, it really has. The tension at home is definitely there right now. She just feels like she can’t count on me when it comes to my cousin’s care, which makes her feel like she has to do it all herself. And I feel bad about it, I really do. My cousin is a good guy. He actually helped me build the deck on the back of our house a few summers ago. We used that composite decking material? It’s supposed to last forever and you never have to stain it. Though, honestly, it gets incredibly hot in the afternoon sun. You can't even walk out there barefoot in July or you'll burn your soles right off. We ended up having to buy this massive outdoor rug just so the dog could go out the back door without doing a little tap dance. \n\nBut, uh, yeah... it definitely affects our relationship. Her being frustrated about the cousin situation, and then seeing me have to constantly double-check everything or redo my prep work for those catering tasks so I don't mix up the small jobs... it just adds to the stress between us, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have trouble following conversations if they were about topics that really interest you?\n\nPatient: Well, that's a good question. I just kind of shrug it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself not caring about the topics your wife is discussing when you have trouble following her?\n\nPatient: Not caring? Oh, no, it's not that I don't care. I love my wife, and I want to hear what she has to say. It's just... I don't know, sometimes my brain just kind of takes a detour. Can you imagine just sitting around that long? I'd go stir crazy. Found a box of old vinyl records I thought I'd lost in the move. Had my original pressing of Abbey Road in there. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, she gets upset because I miss things she tells me about his schedule. The tension at home is definitely there right now. She just feels like she can’t count on me when it comes to my cousin’s care, which makes her feel like she has to do it all herself. And I feel bad about it, I really do. My cousin is a good guy. He actually helped me build the deck on the back of our house a few summers ago. We used that composite decking material? It’s supposed to last forever and you never have to stain it. Though, honestly, it gets incredibly hot in the afternoon sun. You can't even walk out there barefoot in July or you'll burn your soles right off. We ended up having to buy this massive outdoor rug just so the dog could go out the back door without doing a little tap dance. \n\nBut, uh, yeah... it definitely affects our relationship. Her being frustrated about the cousin situation, and then seeing me have to constantly double-check everything or redo my prep work for those catering tasks so I don't mix up the small jobs... it just adds to the stress between us, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have trouble following conversations if they were about topics that really interest you?\n\nPatient: Well, that's a good question. Look, Doc, I... I really don't know how else to say it. I just don't know. I haven't paid attention to whether my mind wanders less if it's something I care about. It's just not something I track. I mean, maybe? But I couldn't tell you for sure.

You can't even walk out there barefoot in July or you'll burn your soles right off. We ended up having to buy this massive outdoor rug just so the dog could go out the back door without doing a little tap dance. \n\nBut, uh, yeah... it definitely affects our relationship. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, clearly and slowly... I mean, maybe? I honestly couldn't tell you. I haven't really paid attention to whether it helps if someone talks like that. It's kind of like... well, my brother-in-law, Frank, he talks real slow. But that's mostly because he's from down south, Alabama originally. He's got this drawl that just stretches every word out like saltwater taffy. We went down to visit them a couple years back, drove the whole way. Took us two days. We stopped at this diner in Tennessee, right off the interstate, and they had the best biscuits and gravy I've ever had in my life. The gravy was real thick, lots of sausage in it, not that watery stuff you get at the chain restaurants up here. Frank tried to tell me how to make it, but he takes so long to get to the point, I think I missed half the recipe. He was going on about the type of flour his grandmother used... 

Anyway, what I'm saying is, even if someone talks slow, I just don't know if I focus any better. I really haven't noticed.
